My hands start shaking, and all I can think is, “Get inside, get away from it.”\n\nIt’s not like I have time to reason with myself. I can be perfectly fine one minute, carrying something or looking out the window, and then that buzzing sound happens and my whole body reacts before I even think. I’ll drop what I’m doing or back away, and I feel like I have to get indoors right away. It feels immediate, like my body is sounding an alarm.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other insects besides wasps?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t love bugs in general, but it’s not the same thing. A spider in the corner or a beetle on the sidewalk might make me wrinkle my nose, but I can usually deal with that.\n\nWith wasps, it’s different. I know, logically, it’s just a little insect, and I know most people would just swat it away or step back and not make a whole production of it. But for me, if one gets near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen. Like my mind jumps straight to danger, even if I’m telling myself, “It’s tiny, it’s outside, it’s probably not even interested in you.” It doesn’t matter in the moment. It feels terrible, like I have to get away before something bad happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities that you avoid because of fear or anxiety, besides those related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of the wasp thing. I mean, I’m here because it’s gotten to where my whole summer feels like it revolves around avoiding them. I’ve stopped doing the garden work at church, which I used to help with, because I just can’t stand the thought of being out there and having one come near me.\n\nAnd even at home, if I hear buzzing by the back porch, I won’t go out. I’ll ask my husband to unload things or handle whatever needs doing out there, even if it’s something I should be able to do myself. So it’s not that I’m avoiding all kinds of places or activities generally — it’s really the situations where I think wasps might be around.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being stung by wasps or is it something else about them that frightens you?\n\nPatient: I suppose the sting is part of it, yes, because nobody wants to be stung, but it feels bigger than just that. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away or step back and not think much more about it. But when one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I can hear how silly it sounds when I say it out loud. It’s not like I’m sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, that might sting me.” It’s more like my whole mind jumps straight to danger, like this awful thing is right there and I have to get away from it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have recurring thoughts about wasps that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll think about them if I know I’m going somewhere they might be, like if someone says we’re eating outside or I see the weather getting warm and people are putting food out. Then I might start thinking, “Oh Lord, what if there are wasps around?” But it’s more tied to the situation, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t just sit at home in the evening and have wasp thoughts coming into my head over and over for no reason. It’s not like I’m trying to push an image out of my mind all day. It’s more that if something reminds me of them, or I hear buzzing, or I’m about to go outside, then my mind goes there pretty quickly. But recurring thoughts out of nowhere… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to any other animal, not just insects?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not especially fond of snakes or mice or things like that, but I think a lot of people aren’t, you know? If I saw a mouse in the house I’d probably holler and want my husband to deal with it, but it’s not that same feeling.\n\nWith wasps it’s different. It’s like my body reacts before I even have time to think. With other animals, I might be startled or uncomfortable, but I don’t usually feel that same urgent need to get away right that second. So no, I can’t really think of another animal that does that to me the way wasps do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more of the wasp itself or of being stung by it?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I suppose, but if I’m being honest, it feels more like the wasp itself. The sting is part of what I’m afraid of, of course, but it’s not just a sensible thought like, “Oh, that might hurt.” It’s the moment I see one near me, my whole body seems to read the wasp as danger.\n\nI know, logically, it’s just a little insect. Most people would maybe wave it away or move their drink and carry on. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen right then, like I need to get away from it before I can even think properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps triggered by seeing pictures or videos of them, or is it only when you encounter them in real life?\n\nPatient: It’s much more when it’s real, when one is actually near me. A picture or a video might make me uncomfortable, like I’ll look away or feel a bit bothered, but it doesn’t hit me the same way.\n\nThe real thing is different because it’s the movement and the sound, I think. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. It’s not even a long thought process. I don’t sit there thinking, “Is that a wasp, could it sting me?” It’s just—there it is, and my body says get away now.\n\nSo no, pictures aren’t really the main problem. It’s when I encounter them in real life, especially if they’re close to me or flying around where I’m standing.\n\nDoctor: Is it the way the wasp looks or its movement and buzzing sound that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: It’s more the movement and the buzzing, definitely. I mean, I don’t like the way they look either, that skinny body and the yellow and black, but the real trigger is when it’s moving around me.\n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, it’s like my body reacts before I can even sort out what I’m seeing. My heart jumps, my hands start shaking, and I just feel this strong need to get inside right away. It’s not really me calmly thinking, “That’s a wasp.” It’s more like the sound or the way it darts around sets me off immediately.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of being stung that scares you most when you encounter a wasp?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I mean, I do think about being stung, and I don’t want that to happen, obviously. But it’s not only that specific thought that scares me most.\n\nIt’s more that when I see a wasp near me, I just feel like something awful is about to happen. I know it sounds out of proportion, because it’s just a little insect and other people would probably swat it away or step aside and not make a big deal of it. But for me, if it comes anywhere near me, my mind and body react like I’m in real danger right then. The sting is part of it, but the fear feels bigger than just “that might hurt.”\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to a specific type, such as yellowjackets or hornets, or is it any wasp in general?\n\nPatient: It’s not really one kind, no. I don’t think I’m calm with some and only afraid of yellowjackets or hornets or anything like that. If I recognize it as a wasp—or even if I’m pretty sure it’s one—that’s enough.\n\nI know, logically, a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away or move aside and not think much more about it. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s more any wasp in general, not a specific type.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, more than I like to admit. I wouldn’t say people have stopped talking to me or anything dramatic like that, but it has changed how I spend time with them.\n\nI used to be part of the church gardening group, and that was one of my main ways of seeing people and feeling useful, you know? We’d be out there planting, weeding, talking while we worked, and I really enjoyed that. But because of the wasps, I’ve had to step back from it. I just couldn’t handle being out there when they were around the flowers or the beds, and after a while it became easier to stop going than to keep making excuses or getting upset.\n\nAnd I also stopped hosting little garden days at my house. I used to have people over, family or friends, and we’d work in the yard or sit outside afterward. That was a big part of how I stayed connected with people. Now I avoid planning things like that, because I’m worried a wasp will show up and I’ll react badly or have to run inside.\n\nAt home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I’m grateful, of course, but I don’t like feeling like I’ve handed over parts of my own home life because I can’t manage them anymore. So yes, it has affected relationships, mostly by making me pull back from people and depend on my husband more.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or situations besides gardening and spending time on your porch due to your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten bigger than just the garden or the porch, honestly. I plan a lot of my summer around whether I think wasps might be around, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what I do.\n\nIf there’s something that needs to be done outside or near the back porch, I’ll try to time it for when I think they won’t be active, or I’ll avoid it altogether. And if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I just freeze up or go back inside. There have been times when we’ve brought things home—groceries, supplies, whatever—and if I hear that buzzing sound or think I saw one, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me doing it. I feel bad about that, because it’s not like I’m incapable of carrying bags, but in that moment I just can’t make myself stay there.\n\nSo yes, it’s not only gardening. I’m here because it’s gotten to where I’m arranging my whole summer around avoiding them, and that’s not how I want to be living.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps affect your daily routine during other times of the year, not just during summer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice it much more in the warmer months, because that’s when I expect them to be around and I’m outside more. During the colder parts of the year, I don’t think my routine changes in the same way.\n\nI mean, I’m probably still aware of it in the back of my mind if I’m thinking ahead to spring or summer, but day to day in winter or when it’s cold, I can’t say it affects me the same way. It’s really when there’s a realistic chance I might run into one that I start planning around it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’m here because it’s gotten to where I’m arranging my whole summer around avoiding them, and that’s not how I want to be living.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps affect your daily routine during other times of the year, not just during summer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice it much more in the warmer months, because that’s when I expect them to be around and I’m outside more. During the colder parts of the year, I don’t think my routine changes in the same way.\n\nI mean, I’m probably still aware of it in the back of my mind if I’m thinking ahead to spring or summer, but day to day in winter or when it’s cold, I can’t say it affects me the same way. It’s really when there’s a realistic chance I might run into one that I start planning around it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever worry about wasps during the winter months when you're not seeing them?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no—not in the same way. In winter, if I’m not seeing them and I’m not hearing them, I don’t usually sit around worrying that one is going to appear.

I might have a passing thought now and then, like if the weather starts changing or I’m thinking about what needs doing outside when spring comes, but it’s not something that takes over my day in the winter. It’s more like… I know the problem is still there, but it’s quiet because I’m not being faced with them. So I wouldn’t say I’m actively worrying about wasps in the winter months, at least not much.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.248\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is that this thing with wasps has gotten bigger than I can manage. I don’t mean I just don’t like them — nobody likes getting stung — but I’ve gotten to where I’m planning my whole summer around avoiding them. \n\nI used to help with garden jobs at church, you know, pulling weeds, tidying up outside, that sort of thing, and I’ve basically given that up because I’m afraid there’ll be wasps around. And at home, if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I won’t even go out there. I’ll have my husband unload things instead, even if it’s inconvenient or silly-looking. It’s embarrassing, honestly, but it’s running more of my life than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic or anxiety attacks when you encounter wasps or think you might encounter them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I don’t know if I’d call them attacks exactly, but it comes on so fast. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering around the flowers, my heart just sort of jumps into my throat. My hands start shaking, and all I can think is, “Get inside, get away from it.”\n\nIt’s not like I have time to reason with myself. I can be perfectly fine one minute, carrying something or looking out the window, and then that buzzing sound happens and my whole body reacts before I even think. I’ll drop what I’m doing or back away, and I feel like I have to get indoors right away. It feels immediate, like my body is sounding an alarm.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other insects besides wasps?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t love bugs in general, but it’s not the same thing. A spider in the corner or a beetle on the sidewalk might make me wrinkle my nose, but I can usually deal with that.\n\nWith wasps, it’s different. I know, logically, it’s just a little insect, and I know most people would just swat it away or step back and not make a whole production of it. But for me, if one gets near me, it feels like something awful is about to happen. Like my mind jumps straight to danger, even if I’m telling myself, “It’s tiny, it’s outside, it’s probably not even interested in you.” It doesn’t matter in the moment. It feels terrible, like I have to get away before something bad happens.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other situations or activities that you avoid because of fear or anxiety, besides those related to wasps?\n\nPatient: No, not really outside of the wasp thing. I mean, I’m here because it’s gotten to where my whole summer feels like it revolves around avoiding them. I’ve stopped doing the garden work at church, which I used to help with, because I just can’t stand the thought of being out there and having one come near me.\n\nAnd even at home, if I hear buzzing by the back porch, I won’t go out. I’ll ask my husband to unload things or handle whatever needs doing out there, even if it’s something I should be able to do myself. So it’s not that I’m avoiding all kinds of places or activities generally — it’s really the situations where I think wasps might be around.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of being stung by wasps or is it something else about them that frightens you?\n\nPatient: I suppose the sting is part of it, yes, because nobody wants to be stung, but it feels bigger than just that. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away or step back and not think much more about it. But when one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I can hear how silly it sounds when I say it out loud. It’s not like I’m sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, that might sting me.” It’s more like my whole mind jumps straight to danger, like this awful thing is right there and I have to get away from it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have recurring thoughts about wasps that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll think about them if I know I’m going somewhere they might be, like if someone says we’re eating outside or I see the weather getting warm and people are putting food out. Then I might start thinking, “Oh Lord, what if there are wasps around?” But it’s more tied to the situation, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t just sit at home in the evening and have wasp thoughts coming into my head over and over for no reason. It’s not like I’m trying to push an image out of my mind all day. It’s more that if something reminds me of them, or I hear buzzing, or I’m about to go outside, then my mind goes there pretty quickly. But recurring thoughts out of nowhere… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to any other animal, not just insects?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m not especially fond of snakes or mice or things like that, but I think a lot of people aren’t, you know? If I saw a mouse in the house I’d probably holler and want my husband to deal with it, but it’s not that same feeling.\n\nWith wasps it’s different. It’s like my body reacts before I even have time to think. With other animals, I might be startled or uncomfortable, but I don’t usually feel that same urgent need to get away right that second. So no, I can’t really think of another animal that does that to me the way wasps do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more of the wasp itself or of being stung by it?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I suppose, but if I’m being honest, it feels more like the wasp itself. The sting is part of what I’m afraid of, of course, but it’s not just a sensible thought like, “Oh, that might hurt.” It’s the moment I see one near me, my whole body seems to read the wasp as danger.\n\nI know, logically, it’s just a little insect. Most people would maybe wave it away or move their drink and carry on. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen right then, like I need to get away from it before I can even think properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps triggered by seeing pictures or videos of them, or is it only when you encounter them in real life?\n\nPatient: It’s much more when it’s real, when one is actually near me. A picture or a video might make me uncomfortable, like I’ll look away or feel a bit bothered, but it doesn’t hit me the same way.\n\nThe real thing is different because it’s the movement and the sound, I think. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. It’s not even a long thought process. I don’t sit there thinking, “Is that a wasp, could it sting me?” It’s just—there it is, and my body says get away now.\n\nSo no, pictures aren’t really the main problem. It’s when I encounter them in real life, especially if they’re close to me or flying around where I’m standing.\n\nDoctor: Is it the way the wasp looks or its movement and buzzing sound that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: It’s more the movement and the buzzing, definitely. I mean, I don’t like the way they look either, that skinny body and the yellow and black, but the real trigger is when it’s moving around me.\n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, it’s like my body reacts before I can even sort out what I’m seeing. My heart jumps, my hands start shaking, and I just feel this strong need to get inside right away. It’s not really me calmly thinking, “That’s a wasp.” It’s more like the sound or the way it darts around sets me off immediately.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of being stung that scares you most when you encounter a wasp?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I mean, I do think about being stung, and I don’t want that to happen, obviously. But it’s not only that specific thought that scares me most.\n\nIt’s more that when I see a wasp near me, I just feel like something awful is about to happen. I know it sounds out of proportion, because it’s just a little insect and other people would probably swat it away or step aside and not make a big deal of it. But for me, if it comes anywhere near me, my mind and body react like I’m in real danger right then. The sting is part of it, but the fear feels bigger than just “that might hurt.”\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to a specific type, such as yellowjackets or hornets, or is it any wasp in general?\n\nPatient: It’s not really one kind, no. I don’t think I’m calm with some and only afraid of yellowjackets or hornets or anything like that. If I recognize it as a wasp—or even if I’m pretty sure it’s one—that’s enough.\n\nI know, logically, a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away or move aside and not think much more about it. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s more any wasp in general, not a specific type.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, more than I like to admit. I wouldn’t say people have stopped talking to me or anything dramatic like that, but it has changed how I spend time with them.\n\nI used to be part of the church gardening group, and that was one of my main ways of seeing people and feeling useful, you know? We’d be out there planting, weeding, talking while we worked, and I really enjoyed that. But because of the wasps, I’ve had to step back from it. I just couldn’t handle being out there when they were around the flowers or the beds, and after a while it became easier to stop going than to keep making excuses or getting upset.\n\nAnd I also stopped hosting little garden days at my house. I used to have people over, family or friends, and we’d work in the yard or sit outside afterward. That was a big part of how I stayed connected with people. Now I avoid planning things like that, because I’m worried a wasp will show up and I’ll react badly or have to run inside.\n\nAt home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I’m grateful, of course, but I don’t like feeling like I’ve handed over parts of my own home life because I can’t manage them anymore. So yes, it has affected relationships, mostly by making me pull back from people and depend on my husband more.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or situations besides gardening and spending time on your porch due to your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten bigger than just the garden or the porch, honestly. I plan a lot of my summer around whether I think wasps might be around, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what I do.\n\nIf there’s something that needs to be done outside or near the back porch, I’ll try to time it for when I think they won’t be active, or I’ll avoid it altogether. And if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I just freeze up or go back inside. There have been times when we’ve brought things home—groceries, supplies, whatever—and if I hear that buzzing sound or think I saw one, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me doing it. I feel bad about that, because it’s not like I’m incapable of carrying bags, but in that moment I just can’t make myself stay there.\n\nSo yes, it’s not only gardening. I’m here because it’s gotten to where I’m arranging my whole summer around avoiding them, and that’s not how I want to be living.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps affect your daily routine during other times of the year, not just during summer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice it much more in the warmer months, because that’s when I expect them to be around and I’m outside more. It’s really when there’s a realistic chance I might run into one that I start planning around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about wasps during the winter months when you're not seeing them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—not in the same way. In winter, if I’m not seeing them and I’m not hearing them, I don’t usually sit around worrying that one is going to appear.\n\nI might have a passing thought now and then, like if the weather starts changing or I’m thinking about what needs doing outside when spring comes, but it’s not something that takes over my day in the winter. So I wouldn’t say I’m actively worrying about wasps in the winter months, at least not much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen right then, like I need to get away from it before I can even think properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps triggered by seeing pictures or videos of them, or is it only when you encounter them in real life?\n\nPatient: It’s much more when it’s real, when one is actually near me. A picture or a video might make me uncomfortable, like I’ll look away or feel a bit bothered, but it doesn’t hit me the same way.\n\nThe real thing is different because it’s the movement and the sound, I think. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. It’s not even a long thought process. I don’t sit there thinking, “Is that a wasp, could it sting me?” It’s just—there it is, and my body says get away now.\n\nSo no, pictures aren’t really the main problem. It’s when I encounter them in real life, especially if they’re close to me or flying around where I’m standing.\n\nDoctor: Is it the way the wasp looks or its movement and buzzing sound that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: It’s more the movement and the buzzing, definitely. I mean, I don’t like the way they look either, that skinny body and the yellow and black, but the real trigger is when it’s moving around me.\n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, it’s like my body reacts before I can even sort out what I’m seeing. My heart jumps, my hands start shaking, and I just feel this strong need to get inside right away. It’s not really me calmly thinking, “That’s a wasp.” It’s more like the sound or the way it darts around sets me off immediately.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of being stung that scares you most when you encounter a wasp?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I mean, I do think about being stung, and I don’t want that to happen, obviously. But it’s not only that specific thought that scares me most.\n\nIt’s more that when I see a wasp near me, I just feel like something awful is about to happen. I know it sounds out of proportion, because it’s just a little insect and other people would probably swat it away or step aside and not make a big deal of it. But for me, if it comes anywhere near me, my mind and body react like I’m in real danger right then. The sting is part of it, but the fear feels bigger than just “that might hurt.”\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to a specific type, such as yellowjackets or hornets, or is it any wasp in general?\n\nPatient: It’s not really one kind, no. I don’t think I’m calm with some and only afraid of yellowjackets or hornets or anything like that. If I recognize it as a wasp—or even if I’m pretty sure it’s one—that’s enough.\n\nI know, logically, a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away or move aside and not think much more about it. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s more any wasp in general, not a specific type.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, more than I like to admit. I wouldn’t say people have stopped talking to me or anything dramatic like that, but it has changed how I spend time with them.\n\nI used to be part of the church gardening group, and that was one of my main ways of seeing people and feeling useful, you know? We’d be out there planting, weeding, talking while we worked, and I really enjoyed that. But because of the wasps, I’ve had to step back from it. I just couldn’t handle being out there when they were around the flowers or the beds, and after a while it became easier to stop going than to keep making excuses or getting upset.\n\nAnd I also stopped hosting little garden days at my house. I used to have people over, family or friends, and we’d work in the yard or sit outside afterward. That was a big part of how I stayed connected with people. Now I avoid planning things like that, because I’m worried a wasp will show up and I’ll react badly or have to run inside.\n\nAt home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I’m grateful, of course, but I don’t like feeling like I’ve handed over parts of my own home life because I can’t manage them anymore. So yes, it has affected relationships, mostly by making me pull back from people and depend on my husband more.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or situations besides gardening and spending time on your porch due to your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten bigger than just the garden or the porch, honestly. I plan a lot of my summer around whether I think wasps might be around, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what I do.\n\nIf there’s something that needs to be done outside or near the back porch, I’ll try to time it for when I think they won’t be active, or I’ll avoid it altogether. And if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I just freeze up or go back inside. There have been times when we’ve brought things home—groceries, supplies, whatever—and if I hear that buzzing sound or think I saw one, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me doing it. I feel bad about that, because it’s not like I’m incapable of carrying bags, but in that moment I just can’t make myself stay there.\n\nSo yes, it’s not only gardening. I’m here because it’s gotten to where I’m arranging my whole summer around avoiding them, and that’s not how I want to be living.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps affect your daily routine during other times of the year, not just during summer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice it much more in the warmer months, because that’s when I expect them to be around and I’m outside more. During the colder parts of the year, I don’t think my routine changes in the same way.\n\nI mean, I’m probably still aware of it in the back of my mind if I’m thinking ahead to spring or summer, but day to day in winter or when it’s cold, I can’t say it affects me the same way. It’s really when there’s a realistic chance I might run into one that I start planning around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about wasps during the winter months when you're not seeing them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—not in the same way. In winter, if I’m not seeing them and I’m not hearing them, I don’t usually sit around worrying that one is going to appear.\n\nI might have a passing thought now and then, like if the weather starts changing or I’m thinking about what needs doing outside when spring comes, but it’s not something that takes over my day in the winter. It’s more like… I know the problem is still there, but it’s quiet because I’m not being faced with them. So I wouldn’t say I’m actively worrying about wasps in the winter months, at least not much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes, it has. I’m not working, so it’s not really a job issue for me, but it has affected the things I used to do and enjoy.

The biggest one is the church gardening group. I used to be pretty involved with that. It wasn’t just about flowers or pulling weeds, you know—it was a way I stayed connected with people. We’d talk while we worked, share plants, sometimes make a whole morning of it. I make excuses or just don’t plan them anymore, because the thought of being responsible for people being outside near my porch or garden, and then a wasp showing up, it’s just too much.

At home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden tasks I used to do myself. Things I didn’t think twice about before, I now avoid or ask him to handle. So it’s definitely affected my routines and the parts of my life that helped me feel useful and connected.

But when one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I can hear how silly it sounds when I say it out loud. It’s not like I’m sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, that might sting me.” It’s more like my whole mind jumps straight to danger, like this awful thing is right there and I have to get away from it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have recurring thoughts about wasps that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll think about them if I know I’m going somewhere they might be, like if someone says we’re eating outside or I see the weather getting warm and people are putting food out. Then I might start thinking, “Oh Lord, what if there are wasps around?” But it’s more tied to the situation, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t just sit at home in the evening and have wasp thoughts coming into my head over and over for no reason. It’s not like I’m trying to push an image out of my mind all day. I mean, I’m not especially fond of snakes or mice or things like that, but I think a lot of people aren’t, you know? If I saw a mouse in the house I’d probably holler and want my husband to deal with it, but it’s not that same feeling.\n\nWith wasps it’s different. It’s like my body reacts before I even have time to think. With other animals, I might be startled or uncomfortable, but I don’t usually feel that same urgent need to get away right that second. So no, I can’t really think of another animal that does that to me the way wasps do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more of the wasp itself or of being stung by it?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I suppose, but if I’m being honest, it feels more like the wasp itself. The sting is part of what I’m afraid of, of course, but it’s not just a sensible thought like, “Oh, that might hurt.” It’s the moment I see one near me, my whole body seems to read the wasp as danger.\n\nI know, logically, it’s just a little insect. Most people would maybe wave it away or move their drink and carry on. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen right then, like I need to get away from it before I can even think properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps triggered by seeing pictures or videos of them, or is it only when you encounter them in real life?\n\nPatient: It’s much more when it’s real, when one is actually near me. A picture or a video might make me uncomfortable, like I’ll look away or feel a bit bothered, but it doesn’t hit me the same way.\n\nThe real thing is different because it’s the movement and the sound, I think. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. It’s not even a long thought process. I don’t sit there thinking, “Is that a wasp, could it sting me?” It’s just—there it is, and my body says get away now.\n\nSo no, pictures aren’t really the main problem. It’s when I encounter them in real life, especially if they’re close to me or flying around where I’m standing.\n\nDoctor: Is it the way the wasp looks or its movement and buzzing sound that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: It’s more the movement and the buzzing, definitely. I mean, I don’t like the way they look either, that skinny body and the yellow and black, but the real trigger is when it’s moving around me.\n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, it’s like my body reacts before I can even sort out what I’m seeing. My heart jumps, my hands start shaking, and I just feel this strong need to get inside right away. It’s not really me calmly thinking, “That’s a wasp.” It’s more like the sound or the way it darts around sets me off immediately.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of being stung that scares you most when you encounter a wasp?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I mean, I do think about being stung, and I don’t want that to happen, obviously. But it’s not only that specific thought that scares me most.\n\nIt’s more that when I see a wasp near me, I just feel like something awful is about to happen. I know it sounds out of proportion, because it’s just a little insect and other people would probably swat it away or step aside and not make a big deal of it. But for me, if it comes anywhere near me, my mind and body react like I’m in real danger right then. The sting is part of it, but the fear feels bigger than just “that might hurt.”\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to a specific type, such as yellowjackets or hornets, or is it any wasp in general?\n\nPatient: It’s not really one kind, no. I don’t think I’m calm with some and only afraid of yellowjackets or hornets or anything like that. If I recognize it as a wasp—or even if I’m pretty sure it’s one—that’s enough.\n\nI know, logically, a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away or move aside and not think much more about it. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s more any wasp in general, not a specific type.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, more than I like to admit. I wouldn’t say people have stopped talking to me or anything dramatic like that, but it has changed how I spend time with them.\n\nI used to be part of the church gardening group, and that was one of my main ways of seeing people and feeling useful, you know? We’d be out there planting, weeding, talking while we worked, and I really enjoyed that. But because of the wasps, I’ve had to step back from it. I just couldn’t handle being out there when they were around the flowers or the beds, and after a while it became easier to stop going than to keep making excuses or getting upset.\n\nAnd I also stopped hosting little garden days at my house. I used to have people over, family or friends, and we’d work in the yard or sit outside afterward. That was a big part of how I stayed connected with people. Now I avoid planning things like that, because I’m worried a wasp will show up and I’ll react badly or have to run inside.\n\nAt home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I’m grateful, of course, but I don’t like feeling like I’ve handed over parts of my own home life because I can’t manage them anymore. So yes, it has affected relationships, mostly by making me pull back from people and depend on my husband more.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or situations besides gardening and spending time on your porch due to your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten bigger than just the garden or the porch, honestly. I plan a lot of my summer around whether I think wasps might be around, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what I do.\n\nIf there’s something that needs to be done outside or near the back porch, I’ll try to time it for when I think they won’t be active, or I’ll avoid it altogether. And if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I just freeze up or go back inside. There have been times when we’ve brought things home—groceries, supplies, whatever—and if I hear that buzzing sound or think I saw one, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me doing it. I feel bad about that, because it’s not like I’m incapable of carrying bags, but in that moment I just can’t make myself stay there.\n\nSo yes, it’s not only gardening. I’m here because it’s gotten to where I’m arranging my whole summer around avoiding them, and that’s not how I want to be living.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps affect your daily routine during other times of the year, not just during summer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice it much more in the warmer months, because that’s when I expect them to be around and I’m outside more. During the colder parts of the year, I don’t think my routine changes in the same way.\n\nI mean, I’m probably still aware of it in the back of my mind if I’m thinking ahead to spring or summer, but day to day in winter or when it’s cold, I can’t say it affects me the same way. It’s really when there’s a realistic chance I might run into one that I start planning around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about wasps during the winter months when you're not seeing them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—not in the same way. In winter, if I’m not seeing them and I’m not hearing them, I don’t usually sit around worrying that one is going to appear.\n\nI might have a passing thought now and then, like if the weather starts changing or I’m thinking about what needs doing outside when spring comes, but it’s not something that takes over my day in the winter. It’s more like… I know the problem is still there, but it’s quiet because I’m not being faced with them. So I wouldn’t say I’m actively worrying about wasps in the winter months, at least not much.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps caused you any problems at work or affected your ability to participate in hobbies or other activities you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’m not working, so it’s not really a job issue for me, but it has affected the things I used to do and enjoy.\n\nThe biggest one is the church gardening group. I used to be pretty involved with that. It wasn’t just about flowers or pulling weeds, you know—it was a way I stayed connected with people. In winter, if I’m not seeing them and I’m not hearing them, I don’t usually sit around worrying that one is going to appear.\n\nI might have a passing thought now and then, like if the weather starts changing or I’m thinking about what needs doing outside when spring comes, but it’s not something that takes over my day in the winter. It’s more like… I know the problem is still there, but it’s quiet because I’m not being faced with them. So I wouldn’t say I’m actively worrying about wasps in the winter months, at least not much.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps caused you any problems at work or affected your ability to participate in hobbies or other activities you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’m not working, so it’s not really a job issue for me, but it has affected the things I used to do and enjoy.\n\nThe biggest one is the church gardening group. I used to be pretty involved with that. It wasn’t just about flowers or pulling weeds, you know—it was a way I stayed connected with people. We’d talk while we worked, share plants, sometimes make a whole morning of it. But I’ve had to step back from that because I just can’t relax out there anymore if I’m worried about wasps being around. I feel embarrassed even saying that, because it sounds like such a small thing, but it changed something that was important to me.\n\nAnd I used to host little garden days at my house too, where people would come over and we’d work in the garden or sit out after. I stopped doing that. I make excuses or just don’t plan them anymore, because the thought of being responsible for people being outside near my porch or garden, and then a wasp showing up, it’s just too much.\n\nAt home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden tasks I used to do myself. Things I didn’t think twice about before, I now avoid or ask him to handle. So it’s definitely affected my routines and the parts of my life that helped me feel useful and connected.\n\n[Last Question]\nHas your fear of wasps significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 57 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’ve had to step back from the church gardening group and stopped hosting garden days, which used to be a big part of how I stayed connected with people. At home, my husband has taken over porch and garden tasks I used to handle myself.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I would say it has, more than I like to admit. It’s not like every single part of my life is ruined or anything, but it has taken away things that mattered to me.

The church gardening group was a big one. I used to really enjoy that—not just the gardening itself, but being with people, feeling like I was helping, having that regular connection. And I’ve stepped back from it because I just don’t feel comfortable out there anymore. I’m too distracted by the possibility of wasps being around.

And then at home, I stopped hosting those little garden days I used to do. That used to be such a nice thing for me, having people over and spending time outside together. Now I just don’t plan them. My husband has also taken over porch and garden jobs I used to do myself, which makes me feel a bit helpless sometimes.

So yes, I think it has affected my quality of life. Maybe not in a dramatic way from the outside, but for me it’s changed my routines and made my world feel smaller.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what finally got me to come in is that this thing with wasps has gotten bigger than I can manage. I don’t mean I just don’t like them — nobody likes getting stung — but I’ve gotten to where I’m planning my whole summer around avoiding them. \n\nI used to help with garden jobs at church, you know, pulling weeds, tidying up outside, that sort of thing, and I’ve basically given that up because I’m afraid there’ll be wasps around. And at home, if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I won’t even go out there. I’ll have my husband unload things instead, even if it’s inconvenient or silly-looking. It’s embarrassing, honestly, but it’s running more of my life than I want it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have panic or anxiety attacks when you encounter wasps or think you might encounter them?\n\nPatient: Yes… I don’t know if I’d call them attacks exactly, but it comes on so fast. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or if I see one hovering around the flowers, my heart just sort of jumps into my throat. My hands start shaking, and all I can think is, “Get inside, get away from it.”\n\nIt’s not like I have time to reason with myself. I can be perfectly fine one minute, carrying something or looking out the window, and then that buzzing sound happens and my whole body reacts before I even think. I’ll drop what I’m doing or back away, and I feel like I have to get indoors right away. It feels immediate, like my body is sounding an alarm.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other insects besides wasps?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I don’t love bugs in general, but it’s not the same thing. I’ll ask my husband to unload things or handle whatever needs doing out there, even if it’s something I should be able to do myself. I know a wasp is just a little insect, and most folks would probably swat it away or step back and not think much more about it. But when one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I can hear how silly it sounds when I say it out loud. It’s not like I’m sitting there calmly thinking, “Oh, that might sting me.” It’s more like my whole mind jumps straight to danger, like this awful thing is right there and I have to get away from it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have recurring thoughts about wasps that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you mean, I don’t think. I mean, I’ll think about them if I know I’m going somewhere they might be, like if someone says we’re eating outside or I see the weather getting warm and people are putting food out. Then I might start thinking, “Oh Lord, what if there are wasps around?” But it’s more tied to the situation, if that makes sense.\n\nI don’t just sit at home in the evening and have wasp thoughts coming into my head over and over for no reason. It’s not like I’m trying to push an image out of my mind all day. It’s more that if something reminds me of them, or I hear buzzing, or I’m about to go outside, then my mind goes there pretty quickly. I mean, I’m not especially fond of snakes or mice or things like that, but I think a lot of people aren’t, you know? If I saw a mouse in the house I’d probably holler and want my husband to deal with it, but it’s not that same feeling.\n\nWith wasps it’s different. It’s like my body reacts before I even have time to think. With other animals, I might be startled or uncomfortable, but I don’t usually feel that same urgent need to get away right that second. So no, I can’t really think of another animal that does that to me the way wasps do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more of the wasp itself or of being stung by it?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I suppose, but if I’m being honest, it feels more like the wasp itself. The sting is part of what I’m afraid of, of course, but it’s not just a sensible thought like, “Oh, that might hurt.” It’s the moment I see one near me, my whole body seems to read the wasp as danger.\n\nI know, logically, it’s just a little insect. Most people would maybe wave it away or move their drink and carry on. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen right then, like I need to get away from it before I can even think properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps triggered by seeing pictures or videos of them, or is it only when you encounter them in real life?\n\nPatient: It’s much more when it’s real, when one is actually near me. A picture or a video might make me uncomfortable, like I’ll look away or feel a bit bothered, but it doesn’t hit me the same way.\n\nThe real thing is different because it’s the movement and the sound, I think. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. It’s not even a long thought process. I don’t sit there thinking, “Is that a wasp, could it sting me?” It’s just—there it is, and my body says get away now.\n\nSo no, pictures aren’t really the main problem. It’s when I encounter them in real life, especially if they’re close to me or flying around where I’m standing.\n\nDoctor: Is it the way the wasp looks or its movement and buzzing sound that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: It’s more the movement and the buzzing, definitely. I mean, I don’t like the way they look either, that skinny body and the yellow and black, but the real trigger is when it’s moving around me.\n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, it’s like my body reacts before I can even sort out what I’m seeing. My heart jumps, my hands start shaking, and I just feel this strong need to get inside right away. It’s not really me calmly thinking, “That’s a wasp.” It’s more like the sound or the way it darts around sets me off immediately.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of being stung that scares you most when you encounter a wasp?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I mean, I do think about being stung, and I don’t want that to happen, obviously. But it’s not only that specific thought that scares me most.\n\nIt’s more that when I see a wasp near me, I just feel like something awful is about to happen. I know it sounds out of proportion, because it’s just a little insect and other people would probably swat it away or step aside and not make a big deal of it. But for me, if it comes anywhere near me, my mind and body react like I’m in real danger right then. The sting is part of it, but the fear feels bigger than just “that might hurt.”\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to a specific type, such as yellowjackets or hornets, or is it any wasp in general?\n\nPatient: It’s not really one kind, no. I don’t think I’m calm with some and only afraid of yellowjackets or hornets or anything like that. If I recognize it as a wasp—or even if I’m pretty sure it’s one—that’s enough.\n\nI know, logically, a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away or move aside and not think much more about it. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s more any wasp in general, not a specific type.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, more than I like to admit. I wouldn’t say people have stopped talking to me or anything dramatic like that, but it has changed how I spend time with them.\n\nI used to be part of the church gardening group, and that was one of my main ways of seeing people and feeling useful, you know? I just couldn’t handle being out there when they were around the flowers or the beds, and after a while it became easier to stop going than to keep making excuses or getting upset.\n\nAnd I also stopped hosting little garden days at my house. I used to have people over, family or friends, and we’d work in the yard or sit outside afterward. That was a big part of how I stayed connected with people. Now I avoid planning things like that, because I’m worried a wasp will show up and I’ll react badly or have to run inside.\n\nAt home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. So yes, it has affected relationships, mostly by making me pull back from people and depend on my husband more.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or situations besides gardening and spending time on your porch due to your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes. I plan a lot of my summer around whether I think wasps might be around, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what I do.\n\nIf there’s something that needs to be done outside or near the back porch, I’ll try to time it for when I think they won’t be active, or I’ll avoid it altogether. There have been times when we’ve brought things home—groceries, supplies, whatever—and if I hear that buzzing sound or think I saw one, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me doing it. I’m here because it’s gotten to where I’m arranging my whole summer around avoiding them, and that’s not how I want to be living.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps affect your daily routine during other times of the year, not just during summer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice it much more in the warmer months, because that’s when I expect them to be around and I’m outside more. During the colder parts of the year, I don’t think my routine changes in the same way.\n\nI mean, I’m probably still aware of it in the back of my mind if I’m thinking ahead to spring or summer, but day to day in winter or when it’s cold, I can’t say it affects me the same way. It’s really when there’s a realistic chance I might run into one that I start planning around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about wasps during the winter months when you're not seeing them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—not in the same way. In winter, if I’m not seeing them and I’m not hearing them, I don’t usually sit around worrying that one is going to appear.\n\nI might have a passing thought now and then, like if the weather starts changing or I’m thinking about what needs doing outside when spring comes, but it’s not something that takes over my day in the winter. It’s more like… I know the problem is still there, but it’s quiet because I’m not being faced with them. So no, I can’t really think of another animal that does that to me the way wasps do.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear more of the wasp itself or of being stung by it?\n\nPatient: It’s both, I suppose, but if I’m being honest, it feels more like the wasp itself. The sting is part of what I’m afraid of, of course, but it’s not just a sensible thought like, “Oh, that might hurt.” It’s the moment I see one near me, my whole body seems to read the wasp as danger.\n\nI know, logically, it’s just a little insect. Most people would maybe wave it away or move their drink and carry on. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen right then, like I need to get away from it before I can even think properly.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps triggered by seeing pictures or videos of them, or is it only when you encounter them in real life?\n\nPatient: It’s much more when it’s real, when one is actually near me. A picture or a video might make me uncomfortable, like I’ll look away or feel a bit bothered, but it doesn’t hit me the same way.\n\nThe real thing is different because it’s the movement and the sound, I think. The second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, my heart just jumps. My hands start shaking, and I feel like I have to get inside right away. It’s not even a long thought process. I don’t sit there thinking, “Is that a wasp, could it sting me?” It’s just—there it is, and my body says get away now.\n\nSo no, pictures aren’t really the main problem. It’s when I encounter them in real life, especially if they’re close to me or flying around where I’m standing.\n\nDoctor: Is it the way the wasp looks or its movement and buzzing sound that triggers your fear?\n\nPatient: It’s more the movement and the buzzing, definitely. I mean, I don’t like the way they look either, that skinny body and the yellow and black, but the real trigger is when it’s moving around me.\n\nThe second I hear that sharp buzzing by the porch, or I see one hovering near the flowers, it’s like my body reacts before I can even sort out what I’m seeing. My heart jumps, my hands start shaking, and I just feel this strong need to get inside right away. It’s not really me calmly thinking, “That’s a wasp.” It’s more like the sound or the way it darts around sets me off immediately.\n\nDoctor: Is it the possibility of being stung that scares you most when you encounter a wasp?\n\nPatient: Not exactly, no. I mean, I do think about being stung, and I don’t want that to happen, obviously. But it’s not only that specific thought that scares me most.\n\nIt’s more that when I see a wasp near me, I just feel like something awful is about to happen. I know it sounds out of proportion, because it’s just a little insect and other people would probably swat it away or step aside and not make a big deal of it. But for me, if it comes anywhere near me, my mind and body react like I’m in real danger right then. The sting is part of it, but the fear feels bigger than just “that might hurt.”\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of wasps limited to a specific type, such as yellowjackets or hornets, or is it any wasp in general?\n\nPatient: It’s not really one kind, no. I don’t think I’m calm with some and only afraid of yellowjackets or hornets or anything like that. If I recognize it as a wasp—or even if I’m pretty sure it’s one—that’s enough.\n\nI know, logically, a wasp is just a little insect, and most people would probably just swat it away or move aside and not think much more about it. But for me, if one gets anywhere near me, it feels like something terrible is about to happen. So it’s more any wasp in general, not a specific type.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, more than I like to admit. I wouldn’t say people have stopped talking to me or anything dramatic like that, but it has changed how I spend time with them.\n\nI used to be part of the church gardening group, and that was one of my main ways of seeing people and feeling useful, you know? We’d be out there planting, weeding, talking while we worked, and I really enjoyed that. But because of the wasps, I’ve had to step back from it. I just couldn’t handle being out there when they were around the flowers or the beds, and after a while it became easier to stop going than to keep making excuses or getting upset.\n\nAnd I also stopped hosting little garden days at my house. I used to have people over, family or friends, and we’d work in the yard or sit outside afterward. That was a big part of how I stayed connected with people. Now I avoid planning things like that, because I’m worried a wasp will show up and I’ll react badly or have to run inside.\n\nAt home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden things I used to do myself. I’m grateful, of course, but I don’t like feeling like I’ve handed over parts of my own home life because I can’t manage them anymore. So yes, it has affected relationships, mostly by making me pull back from people and depend on my husband more.\n\nDoctor: Have you avoided any other activities or situations besides gardening and spending time on your porch due to your fear of wasps?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten bigger than just the garden or the porch, honestly. I plan a lot of my summer around whether I think wasps might be around, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, but that’s what I do.\n\nIf there’s something that needs to be done outside or near the back porch, I’ll try to time it for when I think they won’t be active, or I’ll avoid it altogether. And if I hear buzzing near the back porch, I just freeze up or go back inside. There have been times when we’ve brought things home—groceries, supplies, whatever—and if I hear that buzzing sound or think I saw one, I’ll make my husband unload things instead of me doing it. I feel bad about that, because it’s not like I’m incapable of carrying bags, but in that moment I just can’t make myself stay there.\n\nSo yes, it’s not only gardening. I’m here because it’s gotten to where I’m arranging my whole summer around avoiding them, and that’s not how I want to be living.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of wasps affect your daily routine during other times of the year, not just during summer?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I notice it much more in the warmer months, because that’s when I expect them to be around and I’m outside more. During the colder parts of the year, I don’t think my routine changes in the same way.\n\nI mean, I’m probably still aware of it in the back of my mind if I’m thinking ahead to spring or summer, but day to day in winter or when it’s cold, I can’t say it affects me the same way. It’s really when there’s a realistic chance I might run into one that I start planning around it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever worry about wasps during the winter months when you're not seeing them?\n\nPatient: Not really, no—not in the same way. In winter, if I’m not seeing them and I’m not hearing them, I don’t usually sit around worrying that one is going to appear.\n\nI might have a passing thought now and then, like if the weather starts changing or I’m thinking about what needs doing outside when spring comes, but it’s not something that takes over my day in the winter. It’s more like… I know the problem is still there, but it’s quiet because I’m not being faced with them. So I wouldn’t say I’m actively worrying about wasps in the winter months, at least not much.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps caused you any problems at work or affected your ability to participate in hobbies or other activities you enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I’m not working, so it’s not really a job issue for me, but it has affected the things I used to do and enjoy.\n\nThe biggest one is the church gardening group. I used to be pretty involved with that. It wasn’t just about flowers or pulling weeds, you know—it was a way I stayed connected with people. We’d talk while we worked, share plants, sometimes make a whole morning of it. But I’ve had to step back from that because I just can’t relax out there anymore if I’m worried about wasps being around. I feel embarrassed even saying that, because it sounds like such a small thing, but it changed something that was important to me.\n\nAnd I used to host little garden days at my house too, where people would come over and we’d work in the garden or sit out after. I stopped doing that. I make excuses or just don’t plan them anymore, because the thought of being responsible for people being outside near my porch or garden, and then a wasp showing up, it’s just too much.\n\nAt home, my husband has taken over a lot of the porch and garden tasks I used to do myself. Things I didn’t think twice about before, I now avoid or ask him to handle. So it’s definitely affected my routines and the parts of my life that helped me feel useful and connected.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of wasps significantly impacted your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I would say it has, more than I like to admit. It’s not like every single part of my life is ruined or anything, but it has taken away things that mattered to me.\n\nThe church gardening group was a big one. I used to really enjoy that—not just the gardening itself, but being with people, feeling like I was helping, having that regular connection. And I’ve stepped back from it because I just don’t feel comfortable out there anymore. I’m too distracted by the possibility of wasps being around.\n\nAnd then at home, I stopped hosting those little garden days I used to do. That used to be such a nice thing for me, having people over and spending time outside together. Now I just don’t plan them. My husband has also taken over porch and garden jobs I used to do myself, which makes me feel a bit helpless sometimes.\n\nSo yes, I think it has affected my quality of life. Maybe not in a dramatic way from the outside, but for me it’s changed my routines and made my world feel smaller."
